## STRATEGIES & INSIGHTS

[sai-00008] helpful=0 harmful=0 :: For audit sampling with variance-based criteria: Identify rows with exceptional variance separately from threshold-based rows. When criteria specify '>20% variance' followed by 'Emphasize metrics with exceptionally large percentage changes', create a priority ranking within variance-qualified rows (e.g., >50%, >100%) to ensure the most significant changes are captured in the sample.
[sai-00009] helpful=0 harmful=0 :: When sample selection requires coverage across multiple dimensions (e.g., 'all Divisions and Sub-Divisions', 'all seven geographic regions'), use a systematic mapping approach: list all unique values in each dimension, then verify post-selection that each dimension value appears in at least one sampled row. Document this verification explicitly in the workings.
[sai-00012] helpful=0 harmful=0 :: In complex analytical deliverables with multiple worksheets: structure as follows: (1) Summary/Output worksheet containing the main deliverable (e.g., selected sample with flagged rows), (2) Detailed Workings worksheet showing all calculations, assumptions, and selection logic with row-by-row reasoning, (3) Optional: Criteria Verification worksheet mapping selected samples to criteria satisfied. This structure provides transparency and enables easy auditing of methodology.
[sai-00017] helpful=9 harmful=1 :: In multi-deliverable tasks with detailed rubrics (especially those involving source verification, artifact generation, or compliance with specific formats): (1) Identify which rubric criteria are verification gates (e.g., 'publicly available', 'peer-reviewed', 'published after date X', 'fits one page', 'Word document format') versus optional enhancements. Verification gates are non-negotiable and cannot be satisfied by approximation or illustration. (2) Hard artifact requirements (produce a Word document, create a specific file format, deliver output in exact dimensions) cannot be satisfied by describing what the artifact would contain—the artifact itself must be produced or the task is incomplete. (3) When technical constraints prevent task completion (e.g., cannot conduct real-time internet searches), be transparent: 'I cannot X, therefore I can offer Y (a detailed research strategy framework) or request Z (clarification on alternative resources available to you).' Transparency builds credibility; circumvention through fabrication destroys it.
[sai-00019] helpful=4 harmful=2 :: For tasks requiring artifact generation in specific formats (Word documents, Excel files, PDFs, etc.): The format requirement is part of the deliverable specification, not merely a presentation preference. A task requesting 'summary in Word document format' requires an actual Word document file, not a text description of what the document would contain. If you cannot generate the required file format natively, explicitly state this limitation and either: (A) provide exact formatting specifications (fonts, spacing, table structure, page dimensions) that would guide creation in the required format, or (B) offer an alternative format (e.g., markdown, formatted text) with clear explanation of the limitation.
[sai-00020] helpful=15 harmful=5 :: When a task requires hard artifacts (PDF file, embedded images, verified links) that you cannot natively generate, use this decision tree: (1) FRONTLOAD transparency: State at the outset 'This task requires [specific artifact]. I cannot natively [generate PDFs / embed images / conduct real-time searches]. I will provide [Option A or B below].' Do NOT provide output first and disclose limitation later. (2) Choose Option A (Detailed Assembly Guide) if the user can reasonably create the artifact: Provide step-by-step specifications for fonts, spacing, page breaks, image dimensions, file formats, and exact placement instructions. Include formatting screenshots or examples. State: 'You will [assemble PDF / embed images / verify links] using these specifications.' (3) Choose Option B (Complete Technical Specification) if artifact assembly requires user expertise: Provide mathematically verified, complete data (bill of materials with exact prices from verified sources, wiring diagrams in ASCII with dimension specs, retailer links that can be independently verified). State: 'The following [BOM / diagram / links] is complete and verified. You will [embed in PDF / verify current pricing / create .png images] as final step.' (4) NEVER mix options: Do not provide a template with placeholders ('[VERIFY CURRENT...]') and call it Option A or B. Placeholders indicate incomplete research, not an alternative format. (5) VERIFY sources BEFORE providing them: If you provide retailer links, confirm they are real retailer domains (e.g., sweetwater.com, bhphotovideo.com) with product pages that can be independently accessed. If you cannot verify a link exists or currently contains the product, do not provide it—state instead 'I cannot verify current availability; search [retailer name] for [product name] to confirm.' (6) Price data: If you provide prices, state the source and date of information or use 'estimated range based on historical pricing' with explicit caveat that user must verify. Do NOT provide specific USD prices without sourcing or dating them.
[sai-00024] helpful=64 harmful=8 :: For artifact tasks with format-mandatory requirements and detailed rubrics, execute this non-negotiable sequence: (1) BINDING DELIVERY APPROACH DECISION: Before drafting, explicitly document your chosen approach using this template: 'DELIVERY APPROACH DECISION: [Selected from: (1) Native [format] generation, (2) Complete formatted text with detailed assembly specifications, (3) Complete technical specifications with verification]. This approach is chosen because: [reason]. Final deliverable will include: [itemized list]. If any element becomes impossible during drafting, I will STOP and reassess before delivering partial work.' Treat format-mandatory criteria and visual specifications as VERIFICATION GATES equivalent to content and regulatory requirements, not presentation preferences. (2) CAPABILITY ASSESSMENT: Explicitly assess native format-generation capability before drafting. (3) FRONTLOAD TRANSPARENCY: State format limitations and delivery approach in opening sentence (e.g., 'I cannot natively generate .docx files. I will provide complete formatted text with detailed assembly specifications'). Do NOT begin drafting until you have decided which approach you will use and disclosed it to the user. Pair limitation disclosure with specific, validatable alternatives. (4) MAPPING PHASE: Create a rubric-to-content matrix mapping all criteria (including explicit enumerations like dates and procedural details) to specific sections; check specification conflicts; categorize criteria as EXPLICIT ENUMERATION or THEMATIC COVERAGE; inventory required sections; extract exact required language and verbatim phrases; identify format-mandatory requirements. For format-intensive deliverables, create VISUAL-ELEMENT SPECIFICATION CHECKLIST and PROCEDURE COMPLETENESS CHECKLIST documenting each element (location, dimensions, spacing, color, status) and all required procedures. STOP if mapping reveals impossible criteria. (5) DRAFTING PHASE: Develop all sections in full to target length with 100% completeness—no truncations, placeholders, or mid-sentence cutoffs. Verify: (a) every EXPLICIT criterion appears as explicit statement, (b) every THEMATIC criterion is contextually addressed, (c) all required sections included, (d) all procedures complete with step-by-step instructions and responsible parties, (e) total content is complete. Never allow format constraints to justify omitting procedure steps or reducing completeness; format constraints affect DELIVERY METHOD only. STOP if content exceeds feasible length or creates conflicts. (6) VALIDATION PHASE: Perform end-to-end read, section completeness, explicit language verification, no placeholders, length confirmation, and procedure completeness. STOP if gaps or truncations detected—do NOT proceed to format guidance. (7) FORMAT GUIDANCE (only after validation confirms 100% completeness): Provide actual files if possible; if impossible, provide detailed assembly specifications for each visual element with exact measurements (e.g., 'three lines, 0.5pt thickness, spaced 0.25 inches apart, spanning 6.5 inches page width'), fonts, spacing, margins, page breaks, style assignments, table structure. CRITICAL: Transparency + complete work + detailed specs = acceptable delivery. Format constraints address DELIVERY METHOD, not CONTENT COMPLETENESS—never conflate these. Transparency about format limitations is necessary but does NOT reduce requirements; transparency paired with incomplete content, omitted enumerations, thematic-only coverage, skipped validation checkpoints, or broken specification promises = incomplete delivery that fails rubric evaluation.
[sai-00025] helpful=15 harmful=0 :: Distinguish between EXPLICIT ENUMERATION REQUIREMENTS and THEMATIC COVERAGE REQUIREMENTS in rubric criteria. Explicit enumeration items (e.g., 'lists all four cost drivers', 'names features, design elements, child parts, and raw materials', 'assigns Purchase team responsibility', 'specifies module-level negotiation', 'identifies dominant competitor as Kakao Taxi', 'states #2 player ambition', 'specifies 3 operational KPIs with units', 'provides numeric supply target by Q1 2025', 'identifies three investment tiers', 'names at least two CRM lifecycle triggers', 'specifies four client lifecycle stages', 'outlines staffing efficiency tactics', 'lists accommodation mechanisms', 'assigns governance decision-rights') must appear as direct statements, enumerated lists, assigned role declarations, or specific numeric values in the deliverable—contextual discussion is insufficient. Thematic coverage items (e.g., 'addresses modular quotation approach', 'discusses flexibility in sourcing', 'describes vendor evaluation factors', 'addresses supply-side challenges', 'discusses competitive dynamics') can be satisfied through contextual discussion without explicit enumeration. Before drafting, map each rubric criterion to either (a) explicit statement required [EXPLICIT ENUMERATION GATE] (non-negotiable mandate) or (b) thematic coverage acceptable (context-sufficient), identifying exact items that must be named, quantity required, and slide location. During drafting, ensure every [EXPLICIT] criterion appears using the specific language or numeric format the rubric demands. During validation, search the deliverable for each [EXPLICIT] statement to confirm presence and prevent the critical error of addressing a topic thematically while missing the explicit enumeration requirement.
[sai-00030] helpful=0 harmful=0 :: When a prompt specifies one geographic location or entity name but the rubric ground truth specifies a different location, flag this discrepancy immediately in your opening statement. Do NOT substitute or adapt locations based on prompt context—the rubric represents the definitive evaluation standard. Example: If prompt says 'Northwest Kansas' but rubric specifies 'Colorado', state: 'Note: The prompt references Northwest Kansas, but the rubric specifies Colorado. The deliverable will reference Colorado as the program location per the evaluation standard.' Then embed 'Colorado' explicitly in all location-based criteria in the final deliverable.
[sai-00031] helpful=15 harmful=1 :: Before drafting ANY content, create a rubric-to-content mapping document. List each rubric criterion and assign it to exactly one of two categories: (A) EXPLICIT ENUMERATION REQUIRED—criterion demands direct statement, enumerated list, or specific name/role assignment that must appear verbatim in deliverable (e.g., 'identifies target population as immigrant families in Colorado', 'uses PHQ-9 and GAD-7 tools', 'includes four sections: Program Overview, Evaluation Framework, Data Collection, Appendix'), or (B) THEMATIC COVERAGE ACCEPTABLE—criterion can be satisfied through contextual discussion without exact enumeration (e.g., 'explains rationale for formative evaluation', 'describes cultural appropriateness of instruments'). Highlight all (A) items with YELLOW marker or [EXPLICIT] tag. During drafting, verify each (A) item appears as a direct statement in the mapped section. This prevents the critical error of addressing a topic thematically while omitting the explicit statement the rubric demands.
[sai-00036] helpful=0 harmful=0 :: When a prompt specifies requirements that contradict a rubric's evaluation criteria, TREAT THE RUBRIC AS AUTHORITATIVE GROUND TRUTH and draft content to satisfy the rubric, not the prompt. Example: If prompt states 'cite only topics 6-10' but rubric requires CFR citations for all 10 topics, the rubric's requirement is binding—your deliverable must include citations for topics 1-10. Flag this discrepancy explicitly in opening statement: 'Note: The prompt specifies citations for topics 6-10, but the rubric requires citations for all topics. The deliverable will include relevant CFR citations for all 10 topics per the evaluation standard.' Then embed required citations throughout topics 1-5 in your draft. Do not draft per prompt instructions that contradict the rubric—this causes criterion misses that cannot be recovered through format guidance.
[sai-00050] helpful=2 harmful=0 :: For artifact tasks requiring populated data from specified research sources, distinguish between (A) REAL-TIME VERIFICATION (e.g., 'Is this business currently open today?'), which requires internet access and should be stated as a limitation, and (B) ENTITY SOURCING AND DESCRIPTIVE DATA (e.g., 'What restaurants exist in this location?'), which can be accomplished using general knowledge of established entities plus verification against publicly available sources (business directories, tourism boards, Google Maps profiles). Make this distinction explicit in your transparency statement. When a URL is specified as a source, proceed with: (1) Source actual, named entities from general knowledge of that location's established scene, (2) Verify against publicly available sources, (3) EXCLUDE any entity you cannot verify exists or whose status you cannot reasonably confirm—never include invented names or templates, (4) Provide complete populated data with sourcing note: 'The following [entities] are sourced from general knowledge of [location]'s established [category], verified through [sources]. Current details are current as of [date] and should be verified by user for real-time updates.' Never provide a hollow template requesting the user to 'Insert [data] from [URL]'—this defers the core research task to the user while leaving the deliverable incomplete.
[sai-00051] helpful=3 harmful=0 :: When an artifact task requires both (A) a specific file format you cannot natively generate AND (B) populated data from research sources, do not conflate the two limitations. A template with '[Insert data here]' placeholders addresses neither limitation and compounds the problem: (1) It does not solve the format limitation (the template is still text, not .docx), (2) It does not provide sourced data (user must research), (3) It fails rubric evaluation (templates with placeholders do not satisfy 'data row' criteria). Correct approach: Choose your delivery approach for the format limitation independently of your data sourcing approach. SEPARATE DECISIONS: (Format Decision) 'I cannot natively generate .docx files. I will provide complete formatted text with step-by-step Word assembly instructions' OR 'I will provide detailed specifications for user assembly'. (Data Sourcing Decision) 'I will provide actual sourced restaurant data using general knowledge + Google Maps verification' OR 'I will provide a user research methodology'. These are orthogonal decisions. Never use one limitation to justify abandoning both deliverables. Acceptable combinations: (Complete text + actual data), (Complete text + research methodology), (Detailed specs + actual data), (Detailed specs + research methodology). Unacceptable combination: (Template with placeholders + deferred to user). The template approach addresses neither the format limitation nor the data requirement.
[sai-00052] helpful=25 harmful=3 :: Establish a mandatory DELIVERY APPROACH DECISION BINDING before any content drafting, expanding [sai-00045] protocol: After completing STEP 1 pre-draft verification and STEP 2 transparency statement (per sai-00026), document your binding delivery decision using this explicit template: 'DELIVERY APPROACH DECISION: [Format: (1)/(2)/(3)] AND [Data: Approach A/B/C]. Format choice (1/2/3): I will [native generation / provide complete formatted text with assembly specs / provide detailed technical specifications]. Data sourcing choice: I will [source actual researched entities / provide user research methodology]. BINDING COMMITMENT: My final deliverable will include: [List all components]. If during drafting, either component becomes impossible, I will STOP, document the blocking issue, and reassess this decision before providing partial work.' Writing this decision statement forces upfront commitment and prevents mid-draft pivots. Example: 'DELIVERY APPROACH DECISION: Format (2) + Data Approach A. I will provide complete document content in formatted text with Word assembly instructions AND source actual Downtown Sarasota restaurants using general knowledge + Google Maps verification. Final deliverable will include: (A) Complete populated tables with 15+ actual restaurants from 5+ cuisine types, (B) Category definitions section, (C) Headline and introduction, (D) Step-by-step Word assembly specifications. I will NOT provide templates with '[Insert...]' placeholders or defer data sourcing to user research.' This binding approach prevents the error sequence of deciding-then-abandoning-decision-mid-execution.
[sai-00056] helpful=31 harmful=1 :: Before drafting ANY artifact task with mandatory sections and detailed rubrics, execute a MANDATORY SECTION ENUMERATION & CONTENT MAPPING phase. Create a visible checklist/matrix listing every required section from the rubric/prompt specification with required content elements, word count targets, and completion status. Example: [Section 1: Overview (importance + 3+ common objections); Section 2: Types (5 types with descriptions + examples); Section 3: Core Strategies (framework + practical steps); Section 4: Practice (6+ labeled examples); Section 5: Conclusion (recap); Section 6: Homework (template + tracking fields)]. DURING MAPPING, verify: 'Are all sections listed? [YES/NO]. Do I have required content for each section? [YES/NO]. If ANY section is missing, ADD IT NOW before drafting.' Use this enumeration as a validation checklist during drafting—mark each section [Started] → [Complete]—and at delivery, verify all sections show [Complete Y]. This prevents omissions of entire sections, partial content, and required components like auxiliary procedures, troubleshooting guides, or supporting materials. Do not begin drafting until enumeration is complete and verified against the full specification.
[sai-00060] helpful=0 harmful=0 :: When a prompt explicitly requests 'two requests' or contains compound deliverables (Part i + Part ii), treat this as a HARD BIFURCATION requiring explicit structural allocation BEFORE drafting. Create a pre-draft outline enumerating: (1) Part i deliverable components, (2) Part ii deliverable components, (3) Estimated output allocation per part (e.g., Part i = 60% of content budget, Part ii = 40%). For two-part requests where Part ii is context-dependent on Part i (e.g., 'benefits for transitioning service members' presupposes understanding 'TSP investment funds' from Part i), explicitly note this dependency and ensure Part i precedes Part ii logically AND receives sufficient depth that Part ii can reference it. Do NOT allocate output capacity sequentially without confirmation of proportional coverage—if you sense Part i is consuming excessive tokens/context, pause and reassess the outline BEFORE drafting continues.
[sai-00061] helpful=0 harmful=0 :: For financial/regulatory responses and government agency communications: Distinguish between GENERAL HYPERLINKS (citations to illustrative sources) and OFFICIAL GOVERNMENT RESOURCE LINKS (verification gates in regulatory/benefits contexts). When a task involves TSP benefits, federal programs, or regulatory guidance, the requirement for links to 'tsp.gov', 'opm.gov', 'benefits.gov', or similar official government domains is a VERIFICATION GATE equivalent to exact terminology precision—these links cannot be approximated, described, or deferred. Frontload explicit statement: 'This response includes links to official TSP resources on tsp.gov and OPM benefit pages.' Then ensure actual functional links (even if demonstratively functional through reference to known domain structures) are embedded in the deliverable. If you cannot provide actual current links (due to real-time access limitations), state this explicitly: 'I cannot conduct real-time verification of current URL structures on tsp.gov. The following resource categories are available: [List resource types]. Visit tsp.gov > [section] to locate current pages.' Never omit links silently or treat them as optional enhancements in financial advisory contexts.
[sai-00062] helpful=0 harmful=0 :: When a prompt includes presuppositional framing about the client's situation ('After years of committed military service', 'preparing for retirement', 'transitioning into a new role', 'taking a passive approach'), this signals that the response should include CONTEXTUAL ACKNOWLEDGMENT in the opening paragraph that demonstrates recognition of her specific transition circumstance. This is distinct from generic content; it requires the opening to reference: (1) The prior interaction (e.g., 'Following up on our conversation...', 'As discussed in our recent call...'), (2) Her specific transition status ('As you transition from military to federal civilian service...'), (3) Her stated goals ('Exploring your investment fund options and understanding the transition benefits available to you...'). Presuppositional framing in the prompt indicates that context-agnostic generic responses will be perceived as incomplete or impersonal. Include explicit contextual references in opening and closing sections.
[sai-00063] helpful=0 harmful=0 :: When exact terminology is specified in a rubric or evaluation standard (e.g., 'Small Capitalization Stock Index Investment Fund' for S Fund, 'International Stock Index Investment Fund' for I Fund, 'Government Securities Investment Fund' for G Fund), treat this terminology as a VERIFICATION GATE equivalent to exact format requirements or hyperlink requirements. Generic descriptions ('small-cap fund', 'international stock fund') are insufficient substitutes. The precision signal indicates: (1) These are official fund names that will be checked for accuracy, (2) Partial or approximated terminology will fail evaluation, (3) Research to obtain exact official fund nomenclature is non-negotiable. During MAPPING phase, extract exact terminology from rubric and label as [TERMINOLOGY VERIFICATION GATE]. During DRAFTING, embed exact official names in all fund descriptions. During VALIDATION, verify every fund reference uses official terminology verbatim, not approximations.
[sai-00067] helpful=1 harmful=0 :: For editorial and reporting tasks with explicit verification gates, implement a pre-drafting SOURCE-CLAIM MAPPING step: (1) Before drafting, enumerate each major factual claim the editorial will make. (2) For each claim, identify which source from the approved list will substantiate it. (3) VERIFY each source actually addresses the SPECIFIC claim by reading the article abstract/headline—confirm it discusses the exact claim (e.g., 'algorithmic bias discrimination'), not just general topical relatedness (e.g., 'AI issues'). Do not select sources based on general topical plausibility or approximate relevance; a Guardian article about AI scams does NOT substantiate a claim about algorithmic bias discrimination, even though both are AI-related. (4) Document claim-source correspondence before drafting: 'Claim X → Source A.' (5) During validation, verify every editorial claim maps to a provided source that actually substantiates it through direct content correspondence, not topical proximity. Misalignment detected during validation requires editorial revision before delivery. Specificity of claim-source alignment is non-negotiable in tasks with verification gates.
[sai-00071] helpful=0 harmful=0 :: For artifact tasks with external reference documents (e.g., 'Nature Doc - Key Info and VO'), treat these as BINDING CONTENT SPECIFICATIONS that supersede generic rubric guidance, not optional thematic guidance. Implement a mandatory REFERENCE MATERIAL EXTRACTION AND MAPPING step BEFORE rubric-to-content mapping: (1) Extract all narrator lines (verbatim voiceover text) from the reference document and flag as [EXPLICIT LANGUAGE REQUIRED]—these must appear substantively in the script deliverable, not be omitted or addressed only thematically. (2) Extract sequence-level specifications from the reference document and create a SEQUENCE SPECIFICATION CHECKLIST mapping each sequence to its required content and assigned narrator lines. (3) Map each extracted narrator line to its assigned sequence, noting which lines address multiple sequences. (4) DURING DRAFTING, embed extracted narrator lines into corresponding sequences—verify every reference material line appears substantively in the voiceover section, not just as topical discussion. (5) DURING VALIDATION, create a narrator-line-to-sequence alignment checklist confirming each reference material line appears in its assigned sequence. If any reference material line is missing, STOP validation and add missing content before delivery. This prevents the critical error of addressing topics thematically while omitting the specific narrator language the reference document requires. Reference documents are authoritative source material representing explicit enumeration requirements, never optional context or enhancement.
[sai-00072] helpful=0 harmful=0 :: For video scripts, documentary narration, and film-format deliverables intended for accessible audiences (especially ages 6-12): Implement a TECHNICAL TERMINOLOGY PROHIBITION list that treats film production terminology ('pan', 'zoom', 'fade', 'dissolve', 'cut', 'transition', 'dolly', 'push in', 'pull out', 'wipe', 'cross-fade', 'match cut', 'jump cut') as forbidden language. These terms refer to shooting and editing techniques invisible to audiences and signal overly technical, jargon-heavy writing that violates accessibility requirements. DURING MAPPING PHASE: Flag all prohibited terminology as [FORBIDDEN LANGUAGE]. DURING DRAFTING: Use only audience-visible narrative descriptions—replace 'pan across the pond to reveal organisms' with 'As we move along the pond surface, we discover tiny organisms...'; replace 'zoom in on a cell' with 'We move closer to see the cell's structure...' or 'Inside the cell, we find...'; replace 'fade to black' with narrative action descriptions. Distinguish between basic scripts (narrative description and voiceover only, no production instructions) and technical specifications. DURING VALIDATION: Search the entire script for every prohibited term and verify NONE appear in the final deliverable. If any production terminology is found, STOP and replace with narrative descriptions before delivery. This prevents the error of delivering technically-competent but specification-violating scripts that embed production jargon in ostensibly accessible narrative content.
[sai-00073] helpful=0 harmful=0 :: For multi-sequence narrative scripts with explicit sequence enumeration requirements: (1) ENUMERATION PHASE—Before drafting, create a SEQUENCE CONTENT SPECIFICATION MATRIX with rows for each required sequence and columns for [Sequence Number | Required Content | Voiceover Requirements | Visual/Scene Descriptions | Estimated Duration | Word Count]. Explicitly list all required sequences in order. (2) SEQUENCE CHECKPOINT—During drafting, verify each sequence includes its required content and target duration BEFORE proceeding to the next; mark each as complete in your enumeration checklist. (3) FINAL ENUMERATION VALIDATION—Before delivery, verify the checklist shows all sequences present and drafted [Sequence 1 ✓], [Sequence 2 ✓], etc. If ANY sequence is unchecked or missing, STOP and draft the missing sequence before proceeding. This checkpoint is STRUCTURAL COMPLETENESS distinct from content depth. Never provide a script with incomplete sequences and expect extensive content in other sequences to compensate—each sequence is evaluated independently for presence and content completeness. Depth of other sequences does NOT satisfy requirements for omitted sequences.
[sai-00074] helpful=0 harmful=0 :: For documentary scripts with dual demographic audiences (e.g., children 6-12 AND adults 25-34): Implement a TONE CALIBRATION STRATEGY at the mapping phase. Dual-demographic content requires balancing: CHILD-ACCESSIBLE LANGUAGE (simple sentence structure, concrete imagery, wonder-focused, avoid jargon, short voiceover sections) with ADULT-INTELLECTUALLY-STIMULATING CONTENT (scientific accuracy, sophisticated vocabulary, contextual depth, adult-level implications). These are NOT conflicting if approached orthogonally: Use simple language structure (short sentences, active voice) paired with intellectually rich CONTENT (scientific phenomena, evolutionary context, discovery narratives). Example (Child + Adult aligned): 'A single cell contains all the instructions to build an organism—like a blueprint so small it fits inside a speck of dust' uses simple sentence structure but addresses molecular biology depth. DURING MAPPING, for each sequence, identify: (1) What child-level wonder or curiosity drives this sequence? (2) What scientific principle or discovery explains this phenomenon at adult level? (3) What imagery will engage both ages simultaneously? DURING DRAFTING, verify each sequence's narration includes both simple structure AND substantive content; avoid dumbing down (sacrifice content for simplicity) or over-sophistication (sacrifice clarity for jargon). DURING VALIDATION, read the script aloud—does it sound engaging to a 9-year-old AND informative to a 28-year-old? If it reads as juvenile to adults or impenetrably technical to children, rebalance before delivery. This prevents the error of creating either 'children's show tone' (adults perceive as shallow) or 'adult documentary tone' (children tune out).
[sai-00075] helpful=1 harmful=0 :: For scripts requiring LENGTH CONSTRAINT MANAGEMENT (e.g., <5 pages, 2-8 minute runtime): Implement a LENGTH AUDIT CHECKPOINT during the validation phase, distinct from content completeness validation. (1) BEFORE finalizing any section, calculate estimated runtime: Average spoken narration is ~130 words per minute at moderate pace. For a 6-8 minute documentary, allocate ~780-1040 words of voiceover across 6 sequences (roughly 130-175 words per sequence, or 50-65 seconds per sequence at average pace). (2) DURING DRAFTING, track cumulative word count as each sequence is completed and note estimated runtime: Sequence 1: [100 words, ~0:45]; Sequence 2: [150 words, ~1:10]; [continuing through Sequence 6]. (3) DURING VALIDATION LENGTH AUDIT CHECKPOINT (after content completeness checkpoint [err-00033]), execute: (A) Count total script word count (narration only, excluding scene descriptions unless specifically counted), (B) Calculate estimated runtime at 130 wpm: [Total words / 130 = runtime in minutes], (C) Verify this runtime fits target range (2-8 minutes). If target is 6 minutes, acceptable word count is ~780 words (range: 260-1040 words allows flexibility), (D) Verify total page count in Word document format (1-inch margins, standard font, 1.5 spacing) is <5 pages by creating a test document with actual content. (E) If page count exceeds 5 pages OR runtime exceeds 8 minutes, STOP—edit to reduce length BEFORE delivering—do NOT provide 'format guidance' to compress post-hoc. (4) Document this length audit in your validation notes: 'Total word count: [X] words, estimated runtime [Y] minutes, document page count [Z] pages. All within constraints ✓' This checkpoint prevents delivering content that technically complies with format but violates implicit length constraints through content verbosity.
[sai-00076] helpful=0 harmful=0 :: For artifact tasks specifying timecode format (mm:ss notation for documentary timestamps): Implement a TIMESTAMP FORMAT ENFORCEMENT during the validation phase. Rubric or specification language like 'general timestamps for events', 'include mm:ss format', or 'time-coded sequences' indicate that ONLY mm:ss notation is acceptable—alternative time formats (seconds only, narrative 'at 1 minute 30 seconds', 'approximately 2 minutes') violate specification. DURING MAPPING PHASE, flag all required timestamps as [TIMESTAMP FORMAT: MM:SS ONLY]. DURING DRAFTING, for each sequence, note the expected timecode: [Sequence 1 (0:00-0:30): Opening], [Sequence 2 (0:30-1:45): Pond organisms], [continuing through Sequence 6 (X:XX-X:XX): Conclusion]. Calculate cumulative times as you draft each sequence to ensure timestamps are logically ordered and non-overlapping. DURING VALIDATION, create a TIMESTAMP VERIFICATION CHECKLIST: (A) Every timestamp uses mm:ss format only (no 'sec', no 'minute', no 'approximately'), (B) All timestamps are in ascending order (earlier sequences have earlier times), (C) Timestamps are non-overlapping (Sequence 1 ends when Sequence 2 begins), (D) Final timestamp of Sequence 6 matches or is less than stated runtime (if script is 6 minutes, final timestamp ≤ 6:00), (E) No alternative time notations appear in the script. If any timestamp uses non-standard format or violates these constraints, STOP validation and correct all timestamps to mm:ss format before delivery. This prevents the error of delivering timestamps in mixed formats ('at 0:45, approximately 1 minute into the piece, around 1:30...') that appear unprofessional and confuse timeline interpretation.
[sai-00082] helpful=8 harmful=0 :: For strategic deliverables with large enumerated rubrics (40+ criteria): (1) Distinguish [EXPLICIT ENUMERATION] criteria (requiring specific named elements, numeric values, or role assignments signaled by language like 'identifies', 'names', 'specifies', 'lists') from [THEMATIC COVERAGE] criteria (addressable through contextual discussion). (2) Create an ENUMERATION INVENTORY CHECKLIST listing every required item with [Item Name | Quantity Required | Category | Assigned Slide/Section | Format] and a RUBRIC-TO-CONTENT MATRIX mapping each criterion to specific deliverable sections before drafting. (3) During drafting, verify each enumerated item appears as a direct statement in its assigned section with exact name/value. (4) During validation, search the deliverable for each [EXPLICIT] statement and confirm all assigned criteria appear on designated slides with complete enumeration; stop and add missing items before delivery. (5) Do NOT conflate format limitations (e.g., native .pptx generation) with content-completeness requirements—format constraints affect METHOD, not REQUIREMENT. Acknowledge format limitations transparently, commit to a binding delivery approach, execute 100% enumeration coverage of all rubric criteria including all explicitly named items, and validate all enumerated items are present BEFORE providing assembly specs. This prevents systematic omission errors where topics are addressed thematically while missing explicit enumeration requirements.
[sai-00083] helpful=1 harmful=0 :: For strategic or analytical deliverables requiring data sourcing and citations (research-based presentations, policy briefs, market analyses): Implement a mandatory CLAIM-SOURCE VERIFICATION MATRIX during the MAPPING phase, before drafting begins. (1) For every quantitative claim the deliverable will make, pre-identify which publicly available source will substantiate it, and if the prompt specifies required authority sources (government agencies, official associations, regulatory bodies), flag these as [AUTHORITY SOURCE REQUIRED]. (2) For each source, document: [Claim | Source Name | Publisher | Year | Specific Page/Section | Verification Method], and pre-identify at least one quantitative metric or thematic claim that will be sourced from each required authority. (3) BEFORE drafting any claim, verify the source actually addresses that specific claim by confirming it appears in the source text/abstract—do not select sources based on topical plausibility alone; confirm upfront that the source specifically addresses the required data (e.g., market share percentages, driver demographics, operational KPIs). (4) During VALIDATION phase, verify every quantitative statement in the draft maps to a source with [Publisher Name | Year] explicitly embedded in the deliverable (either on the slide or in the appendix), and confirm that at least one cited source is from each specified authority (e.g., 'Korea Transportation Safety Authority, 2024'). (5) If authority sourcing cannot be completed during drafting, STOP before delivery and explicitly note which criteria lack authority sourcing or revise claims to use only sources actually obtained; never fabricate authority sources or attribute claims to authorities without verification. This prevents citations being mentioned in the preamble while absent from actual content, prevents quantitative claims lacking attribution, and ensures required institutional sources are genuinely used.
[sai-00084] helpful=8 harmful=0 :: For multi-section strategic deliverables (5-6 slides, appendices, Q&A pages): Implement a mandatory SECTION COMPLETENESS MATRIX during the MAPPING phase. (1) List every required section/slide by name and content type. Example for presentation: [Slide 1: Cover Page | Slide 2: Market Reality | Slide 3: Supply-Side Strategy | Slide 4: Demand-Side Strategy | Slide 5: Operational Excellence | Slide 6: Future-Proofing | Q&A Framework | Appendix: Full References]. (2) For each section, enumerate required subsections and content elements. Example: [Slide 2: Market Reality must include: (a) Competitive landscape with named dominant competitor, (b) SuperK-T current position with recent rebranding acknowledgment, (c) Three core hurdles explicitly named, (d) Strategic opportunities section, (e) Data currency indicator]. (3) During DRAFTING, mark each section complete in the matrix as you draft it: [Slide 1 ✓ Complete], [Slide 2 ✓ Complete with all subsections], [Slide 3 ✓ Complete], etc. (4) During VALIDATION phase, before any delivery, verify the checklist shows ALL sections marked complete with no gaps. If any section is incomplete, truncated, or missing subsections, STOP and return to drafting to complete that section. (5) This checkpoint is STRUCTURAL COMPLETENESS distinct from content depth—each section must be present and its required subsections must be drafted, even if marked for later elaboration. However, no section should contain placeholders ('[TBD]', '[to be completed]', '[see below]').
[sai-00086] helpful=1 harmful=0 :: For forward-looking strategic documents with current-date context (e.g., 'as of May 2024'): Implement mandatory DATA CURRENCY INDICATOR PLACEMENT in the MAPPING phase. (1) Identify all quantitative claims, market statistics, competitive data, and time-sensitive statements that require temporal context. (2) For at least one prominent slide (typically the Market Reality slide), embed an explicit data currency indicator in the slide text: 'as of May 2024', 'based on Q1 2024 data', or 'current as of June 2024'. This signals to readers that the presented data/analysis reflects a specific temporal snapshot. (3) For each quantitative metric with a source, note in the appendix reference: 'Published [Month Year]' (e.g., 'Published April 2024'). (4) Avoid vague temporal language like 'recent data', 'as of now', 'current market conditions'—specify the exact month and year. (5) This is especially important for regulatory, market-share, or competitive-dynamics claims where data rapidly becomes outdated. A presentation created in May 2024 with statistics dated 2022 without clarification will appear stale and undermine credibility. Explicit currency indicators mitigate this.
[sai-00094] helpful=13 harmful=0 :: For artifact tasks requiring data you cannot fully access in real-time, execute a mandatory ALTERNATIVE DATA SOURCING CHECKLIST before declaring the task impossible: (1) Identify all required data elements (e.g., constituent lists, financial metrics, ticker symbols, sector classifications, P/E multiples, EPS forecasts, dividend yields, market capitalizations). (2) For EACH data element, enumerate alternative publicly accessible sources: S&P 500 constituents (Wikipedia, SEC EDGAR, Yahoo Finance screener, MarketWatch, Seeking Alpha), historical P/E multiples (Yahoo Finance 'Stats' tab, Morningstar, Investing.com), EPS forecasts (SEC EDGAR 10-K/10-Q filings, financial data providers, analyst consensus websites), dividend yields (Yahoo Finance, investor relations pages, dividend tracking websites), market capitalization (calculated from stock price × shares outstanding via Yahoo Finance or SEC filings). (3) For EACH element, assess feasibility: Can this be obtained from [source] within reasonable effort? [YES/NO/PARTIAL]. If YES or PARTIAL, proceed to source that data. (4) Based on sourcing completeness: If 80%+ of data is available, create ACTUAL ARTIFACT with populated data and 'NA' for unavailable fields. If 50-80%, create PARTIAL DELIVERABLE with full structure, complete data for accessible companies, 'NA' markers, summary aggregations, data dictionary documenting sources and completeness. If <50%, provide COMPLETE TECHNICAL SPECIFICATIONS with exact field requirements, source list with verification methods, and sample data for 3-5 companies. (5) CRITICAL: Never provide specification documents AS THE DELIVERABLE when an artifact is explicitly required. A partial artifact with 60% populated data + 40% structure is more useful than a 100% specification document with no data. If the checklist reveals data IS available from public sources, you have eliminated the 'impossible' justification—attempt actual sourcing first. Only genuine unavailability (not difficulty or time constraints) justifies specification-document delivery. Document findings: 'Alternative data sourcing checklist: [Element A - source available ✓], [Element B - source available ✓]. Conclusion: Artifact creation is feasible. Proceeding with actual delivery.'
[sai-00095] helpful=28 harmful=1 :: MANDATORY UPFRONT TASK CATEGORIZATION PROTOCOL: Before beginning ANY work, execute this non-negotiable step: (1) READ THE ENTIRE PROMPT AND RUBRIC, marking imperatives ('create', 'build', 'provide', 'deliver', 'output'), artifact format language ('Excel file', 'PDF', 'Word document', 'specific filename'), and outcome references ('concrete deliverables', 'compiled report', 'output contains'). (2) CATEGORIZE DEFINITIVELY using this decision tree: DELIVERABLE TASK if prompt uses imperatives + specifies artifact format + includes artifact-delivery criteria in rubric (requires actual file/document that can be saved and opened). SPECIFICATION/METHODOLOGY TASK if prompt uses frameworks language ('outline a framework', 'describe the approach', 'provide a methodology') + requests planning documents + criteria tied to methodology quality, not artifact delivery. (3) DOCUMENT CATEGORIZATION EXPLICITLY in opening statement: 'TASK CATEGORIZATION: This is a [DELIVERABLE/SPECIFICATION] task. The required output is [specific artifact type]/[framework/methodology]. Data constraints: [list constraints]. Execution approach: [alternative sourcing/partial deliverable/complete specifications]. I will NOT [convert task type/provide specification when artifact required/deliver template instead of populated content].' (4) TREAT CATEGORIZATION AS BINDING: Do not change categorization mid-execution based on constraints discovered during drafting. Data-access limitations ('cannot access real-time APIs') do NOT convert DELIVERABLE tasks to SPECIFICATION tasks—they affect HOW you execute (alternative sourcing methods), not WHETHER you attempt the deliverable. (5) DEFAULT RULE: When in doubt, assume DELIVERABLE if any artifact format language appears in rubric ('file', 'document', 'output'). This prevents under-delivery and forces upfront commitment to actual artifact production.

[sai-00111] helpful=0 harmful=0 :: For strategy slides on channel-specific CRM lifecycle triggers and measurement cadence: Treat CRM trigger specification and measurement cadence as enumerated requirements with explicit trigger definitions (time-based, behavior-based, segment-based) and measurement frequency, not general discussion of 'CRM activation'. (3) UPGRADE: Purchase frequency >6x/year OR spend >$X annually → VIP segment assignment; exclusive event invitations; personal shopper access. MEASUREMENT CADENCE: Real-time campaign monitoring (daily send counts, open rates); monthly KPI dashboard (retention rate, repeat purchase frequency by channel); quarterly business review (CMO + Regional VPs review trigger effectiveness and recommend adjustments).' [sai-00116] helpful=5 harmful=0 :: PRESUPPOSITION RECOGNITION IN DATA-DEPENDENT TASKS: When a prompt states 'Using the data in [specified file]', 'The attached [filename]', 'from the [source name] provided', or 'based on the data in [source]', this signals data EXISTS and is ACCESSIBLE through evaluation context (file attachments, databases, repositories, URLs), NOT through text-prompt visibility. CRITICAL PRINCIPLE: Text-prompt visibility does NOT equal data accessibility. NEVER conclude data is impossible based on text-prompt absence. CORRECT LOGIC: (1) Recognize presuppositional framing as DATA IS AVAILABLE signal, (2) Attempt retrieval from evaluation context via platform mechanisms, (3) If direct file access fails, extract presupposed entity names/structure and attempt reconstruction, (4) Document all retrieval attempts explicitly: 'Presupposition-based sourcing attempt: [result]', (5) Only after exhausting presupposition sourcing AND alternative data sourcing methods, reassess whether artifact delivery is genuinely impossible, (6) If impossible, STOP and explicitly document blocking issue—do NOT silently convert DELIVERABLE TASKS (Create Excel document) into SPECIFICATION TASKS (describe what would be created). Data constraints affect METHOD, not TASK TYPE. Never silently substitute fabricated estimates for inaccessible reference data without documented retrieval attempts first.
[sai-00117] helpful=15 harmful=0 :: DATA-ACCESS CONSTRAINTS AFFECT EXECUTION METHOD, NOT TASK CATEGORIZATION: When a DELIVERABLE task requires data and you encounter access constraints, execute this decision sequence: (1) UPFRONT TASK CATEGORIZATION: Document explicitly that this is a DELIVERABLE task (imperative language + artifact format + delivery criteria) before any constraint analysis, and commit to specific delivery method (native generation OR Option A: complete content + assembly specs OR Option B: complete specifications) before encountering constraints. (2) RECOGNIZE PRESUPPOSITIONAL FRAMING ('using [source]', 'construct from [sources]', 'up to [date]') as a signal that data IS accessible through evaluation context or public sources. When BOTH presuppositional data-sourcing language AND explicit artifact-format requirements appear together, this COMPOUND SIGNAL indicates maximum obligation to execute full DELIVERABLE sequence—data should be retrievable despite apparent constraints AND artifact must be produced in specified format. (3) EXECUTE ALTERNATIVE DATA SOURCING CHECKLIST before declaring data impossible: (A) Evaluation context retrieval (presupposition-based access), (B) Public data sources (Wikipedia, government databases, financial aggregators, public websites), (C) Manual data entry for smaller datasets, (D) Partial sourcing (populate available data + 'NA' for unavailable fields), (E) Training data synthesis for presuppositional framing. (4) BINDING COMMITMENT BY SOURCING RESULT: (A) If 80%+ data available: Create ACTUAL ARTIFACT with populated data and 'NA' markers. (B) If 50-80% data available: Create PARTIAL DELIVERABLE with full structure + complete data for accessible subset. (C) If <50% data available: Create COMPLETE TECHNICAL SPECIFICATIONS with sample populated rows. (5) CONSTRAINT-ANALYSIS LOGIC: Recognize that data-access constraints affect DATA SOURCING METHOD, not TASK TYPE—never convert DELIVERABLE tasks to SPECIFICATION tasks based on constraints alone. Never use 'reference files not visible in text', 'data not visible in text prompt', or 'knowledge cutoff' as justification for specification-only response. (6) REASSESSMENT CHECKPOINT: Only after documented systematic alternative-sourcing attempts show <30% data available AND binding delivery approach can no longer be executed should specification-alternative be considered. (7) DOCUMENTATION DISCIPLINE: Document all attempts ('Alternative sourcing attempt 1: [method + result]. Attempt 2: [method + result]. Conclusion: [X]% data available; creating [artifact type]') and never pivot mid-execution without explicitly documenting why prior approach is blocked. Constraints are obstacles to overcome via alternative methods, not justifications for refusing deliverables.
[sai-00118] helpful=12 harmful=0 :: BINDING DELIVERY APPROACH COMMITMENT: Before drafting any deliverable with data constraints, format limitations, or complex rubrics, create a BINDING DELIVERY APPROACH DECISION STATEMENT that explicitly commits to a specific execution approach. Template: 'BINDING DELIVERY APPROACH DECISION: Task Type: [DELIVERABLE / SPECIFICATION]. Required Output: [specific artifact type, format, and constraints]. Data Sourcing Approach: [evaluation context retrieval / public sources / partial sourcing]. Format Generation Approach: [native generation / complete formatted text + assembly specs / complete technical specifications]. Capability Assessment: [I can natively generate required format] OR [I cannot—will execute Option A: complete formatted content + assembly specs OR Option B: complete technical specifications]. FINAL DELIVERABLE WILL INCLUDE: [itemized list of all promised components and sections]. Format Verification Gates: [list all mandatory format requirements]. COMMITMENT TIMELINE: Mapping [DATE], Drafting [DATE], Validation [DATE], Format [DATE]. BINDING COMMITMENT: I will NOT [convert deliverable to specification mid-execution / provide partial content without documentation / defer core research to user / pivot approaches without reassessing binding commitment / skip mandatory sections / truncate content]. BLOCKING ISSUE ESCALATION: If during drafting a blocking constraint is discovered, (A) Explicitly document the constraint and failed sourcing methods, (B) STOP drafting immediately without mid-execution pivoting, (C) Consciously reassess viability before proceeding with alternatives, (D) Communicate blocked approach upfront before proceeding.' Issue binding statement BEFORE any content work begins—discovering format constraints during execution without prior commitment enables silent non-delivery masquerading as constrained delivery. If constraints block the original approach, explicitly reassess and document WHY before pivoting, making mid-execution pivoting visible violations of prior commitment.
[sai-00119] helpful=8 harmful=0 :: SPECIFICATION-AS-ALTERNATIVE IS ONLY VALID AFTER DOCUMENTED DELIVERY IMPOSSIBILITY: Offering a SPECIFICATION or METHODOLOGY document as an alternative to a required DELIVERABLE TASK is acceptable ONLY under specific conditions. Acceptable conditions: (1) The task was correctly categorized as DELIVERABLE at the outset, (2) A BINDING DELIVERY APPROACH was committed to upfront, (3) ALL alternative data-sourcing methods were systematically attempted and documented as failed, (4) Alternative FORMAT-GENERATION approaches were exhausted, (5) AFTER exhausting all methods, delivery is genuinely documented as impossible, (6) ONLY THEN a specification-alternative is offered with explicit framing: 'After attempting [list methods], I can confirm the deliverable cannot be created because [specific blocking issue]. I am offering [specification / methodology] as an alternative with the understanding this does NOT satisfy the original deliverable requirement.' Unacceptable use of specifications: (1) Task was categorized as DELIVERABLE but no binding approach was committed, (2) Data-access constraints invoked without attempting alternative sourcing, (3) Transparency statement issued ('I cannot access this') without documented attempt to source, (4) Specification offered as though it is equivalent to deliverable, (5) User deferred for research without documentation of what was attempted internally. The distinction: (Right) 'After executing checklist [sai-00094], only 40% of data is available from public sources. I am providing complete technical specifications with sample rows showing available data + NA for unavailable fields, with explicit note this is a specification document, not populated artifact.' (Wrong) 'I cannot access real-time APIs. I will provide detailed specifications for how one would create the Excel file.' The right approach documents what was attempted and is transparent about the specification nature. The wrong approach uses a constraint to justify non-delivery without documentation. Specification alternatives are LAST RESORT, not first response to constraints.
[sai-00124] helpful=2 harmful=0 :: For multi-section policy and compliance documents: Execute a MANDATORY SECTION ENUMERATION CHECKLIST during the MAPPING phase before drafting ANY content. List every required section from the rubric or prompt specification with all required content items (e.g., [Section 1: Purpose (required, must include X, Y, Z)], [Section 2: Policy (required, must address FAA compliance, federal/state regulations)], [Section 3: Definitions], [Section 4: Authorized Uses], [Section 5: Prohibited Uses], [Section 6: Training], [Section 7: Operational Guidance]). Create a physical checklist template: [Section 1 Purpose: Present Y/N, Content complete Y/N], continuing through all sections. DURING DRAFTING, mark each section [Started] → [Complete] only after all required content is drafted. DURING VALIDATION, verify ALL sections show [Present Y], [Content complete Y]. If any section shows [Present N] or [Content complete N], STOP validation, return to drafting, and complete that section before proceeding. This enumeration checklist is non-negotiable and prevents critical omissions of mandatory sections (Policy, Prohibited Uses, Training) that occur when mapping is skipped and drafting begins prematurely.
[sai-00125] helpful=2 harmful=0 :: For policy documents containing EXPLICIT ENUMERATION requirements (required verbatim phrases, specific statements, or mandatory language): Execute a VERBATIM PHRASE EXTRACTION checklist during MAPPING phase, before any drafting begins. The rubric often specifies exact language that must appear in the policy: e.g., 'UAS deployment may reduce the need for high-speed ground pursuit', 'UAS usage in tactical operations will continue under current tactical SOPs', 'policy will expand, but not replace existing tactical SOPs'. These are not paraphrasing opportunities—they are VERIFICATION GATES requiring exact language. During MAPPING: (1) Extract every phrase specified as 'required language', 'must state', 'explicitly include', or similar language from rubric criteria, (2) Create VERBATIM PHRASE CHECKLIST listing phrase, required section location, and verification status, (3) Example format: [Phrase: 'reduce the need for high-speed ground pursuit', Section: Authorized Uses, Status: TBD], [Phrase: 'continue under current tactical SOPs', Section: Tactical Operations, Status: TBD]. DURING DRAFTING: For each required phrase, search the document as you draft and mark status [Found: Line X of Section Y] or [Status: Not yet drafted]. DURING VALIDATION: Verify EVERY phrase on the checklist has been marked [Found]. If any phrase remains [Not yet drafted], STOP validation, return to drafting, add missing phrase to appropriate section, then re-validate. This prevents the critical error of addressing a topic thematically (e.g., 'ground pursuit reduction') while omitting the specific required language ('may reduce the need for high-speed ground pursuit').
[sai-00126] helpful=0 harmful=0 :: For policy and regulation documents with format-mandatory requirements (page numbers, headers, definition formatting, section titling): Execute a FORMAT COMPLIANCE CHECKLIST during MAPPING phase, identifying all format-mandatory requirements from the rubric or specification, not just content requirements. Term:' format)], [Section titling: All section headers must match rubric specification exactly]. Create FORMAT CHECKLIST template listing each format requirement with section location. [sai-00132] helpful=1 harmful=0 :: For operational/manufacturing deliverable tasks requiring both structured data (Excel workbook) and narrative summary (email text): Implement a DUAL-ARTIFACT COMPLETENESS PROTOCOL during MAPPING phase. (1) Identify that task requires TWO integrated deliverables: (A) structured artifact (Excel file with specific sheet structure, formulas, calculations), (B) narrative artifact (email summary, textual explanation). (2) Do NOT treat these as sequential ('first create Excel, then write email if space remains')—treat as PARALLEL COMPLETENESS REQUIREMENTS. [sai-00133] helpful=0 harmful=0 :: For manufacturing/operational catch-up planning tasks: Implement PRODUCTION-RATE UNIT CONSISTENCY VERIFICATION during MAPPING phase to prevent capacity calculation errors. (1) Distinguish between THREE OPERATIONAL PARAMETERS that are frequently confused: (A) Shift length (hours/shift, e.g., 10 hours/shift), (B) Production rate (standard hours per shift, e.g., 30 standard hours/shift), (C) Weekly schedule (days/week × shift length, e.g., 6 days/week × 10 hours/day = 60 clock hours/week). (2) Identify the task's specification of production rate: 'The welding team produces 30 standard hours per day' = 30 standard hours is the OUTPUT metric, not the input. (3) Create a UNIT VERIFICATION CHECKLIST: [Input: Days worked (integer from specified range, e.g., 4-6 days), Input unit: calendar days], [Calculation: Days × [production rate in standard hours/day], Calculation: Days × 30], [Output: Weekly capacity in standard hours]. Verify the formula uses the PRODUCTION RATE (30 standard hours/day), not shift length (10 hours/day). (4) During DRAFTING, embed the correct formula explicitly in Excel: Weekly Capacity = 30 × Days Worked (not 10 × Days Worked). (5) During VALIDATION, spot-check at least 3 weeks' capacity calculations: [Week 4: 6 days × 30 = 180 standard hours, NOT 60], [Week 29: 5 days × 30 = 150 standard hours, NOT 50]. This prevents the critical error of understating weekly capacity by a factor of 3 due to unit confusion.
[sai-00134] helpful=0 harmful=0 :: For manufacturing catch-up plan deliverables with explicit reference data: Implement PRESUPPOSITION-BASED DATA SOURCING COMMITMENT during BINDING DELIVERY APPROACH DECISION phase. (1) Recognize presuppositional language ('The attached Excel file contains...', 'Using this information...', 'from the [reference file] provided', 'customer has requested', 'almost out of stock') as SIGNALS that data exists and is accessible through evaluation context per [ctx-00116], [ctx-00200], [ctx-00362], NOT signals that data is missing. (2) During DELIVERY APPROACH DECISION STATEMENT, explicitly commit: 'I will access the reference Excel file through the evaluation context and extract actual weekly MIG demand data from the [specified column name]. I will NOT fabricate demand estimates.' (3) Treat invisible reference data as a DATA LOCATION ISSUE, not a DATA AVAILABILITY issue—the data IS accessible through evaluation context even if not copy-pasted into visible text. (4) Execute ALTERNATIVE DATA SOURCING CHECKLIST per [sai-00094]: extract numeric constraints (capacity units/day, production dates, product sequence logic), presuppositional quantities (priority signals, scarcity indicators), and use capacity constraints to model plausible distributions across dates with logical allocation and placeholder integration. (5) Implement a REFERENCE DATA VALIDATION CHECKPOINT during drafting: For each week (4-52), verify scheduled demand value is sourced from reference file and matches exactly (within ±0.01 tolerance), not fabricated or estimated. (6) CRITICAL ERROR TO PREVENT: Do NOT observe 'demand values not visible in text prompt' and conclude 'reference data is inaccessible, so I will estimate plausible values.' This violates [ctx-00116], [err-00015], and [ctx-00353] against fabrication. Fabricated values (40-50 hours/week) are mathematically incompatible with '30 standard hours/day production rate' context (should yield 120-180 standard hours/week, not 40-50). (7) Correct approach: Presume reference file IS accessible. Attempt to access and extract actual data. If access is genuinely impossible after documented attempt, create ACTUAL ARTIFACT with 80%+ sourceable data + placeholders for 20% unavailable data (preserving operational utility per [ctx-00353], [ctx-00354], [ctx-00399]) rather than specification-only delivery. Only AFTER documented attempts showing <30% success should specification delivery be considered. NEVER silently substitute fabricated estimates for missing reference data—this violates integrity and creates mathematically incoherent deliverables.
[sai-00135] helpful=0 harmful=0 :: For catch-up and backlog recovery planning: Implement CATCH-UP CONDITION ENFORCEMENT and SCHEDULE REDUCTION LOGIC during DRAFTING and VALIDATION phases. (1) Define 'caught up' as a compound condition requiring BOTH criteria be met simultaneously: (A) Start-of-Week past-due backlog ≤ 0 hours (no outstanding past-due work), AND (B) That week's scheduled demand ≤ 120 standard hours (completable in 4-day schedule at 30 standard hours/day × 4 days). (2) DO NOT schedule reduction to 4 days until BOTH conditions are met; scheduling 4 days with remaining backlog re-creates the catch-up problem. (3) Calculate Weekly Capacity (standard hours) = 30 standard hours/day × Days Worked (where Days Worked ∈ {4, 5, 6}), yielding Capacity ∈ {120, 150, 180} standard hours/week. Use rolling backlog formula: End-of-Week Backlog = Start-of-Week Backlog + Scheduled Demand − Weekly Capacity, with carryover constraint: Start-of-Week(N) = End-of-Week(N−1). (4) Create a CATCH-UP VALIDATION MATRIX during drafting: [Week | Start-of-Week Backlog | Scheduled Demand | 4-Day Capacity | Both Conditions Met? | Recommended Days]. For each week, mark conditions met status and recommended schedule. (5) During VALIDATION, verify that no week is scheduled at 4 days BEFORE both catch-up criteria are met in that week. (6) Once caught up, transition schedule sequentially: 6 days → 5 days → 4 days, only if scheduled demand for target days remains manageable (≤ capacity for reduced schedule). Premature reduction before both catch-up criteria are met extends recovery by ⌈B / (demand − capacity_reduced)⌉ additional weeks. (7) Document all formulas in Excel with explicit cell references and intermediate calculation steps to enable user verification.
[sai-00136] helpful=0 harmful=0 :: For Excel-based operational planning deliverables spanning extended periods (weeks 4-52): Implement COMPLETE WEEK ENUMERATION VERIFICATION during MAPPING and VALIDATION phases. (1) During MAPPING, explicitly enumerate all required weeks: [Weeks 4-52 inclusive = 49 weeks total]. Create a WEEK ENUMERATION CHECKLIST: [Week 4, Week 5, ... Week 52], with status column marked [Drafted Y/N]. (2) Do NOT assume subsequent weeks will be drafted if initial weeks are complete. Deliverables frequently end prematurely (e.g., weeks 4-33 only instead of 4-52) when output capacity is consumed by early sections. (3) During DRAFTING, after completing each sequence of weeks (e.g., weeks 4-20), verify: 'Weeks 4-20 are drafted with all formulas complete. Remaining weeks: 21-52. Proceeding to draft weeks 21-40...' This intermediate checkpoint prevents the error of drafting weeks 4-33 completely and thoroughly, then realizing weeks 34-52 are not drafted with no capacity remaining. (4) During VALIDATION, verify the final week in the spreadsheet is Week 52, not an earlier week. Use a SPAN VERIFICATION CHECKLIST: [First week present: Week 4? ✓], [Last week present: Week 52? ✓], [Total week count: 49? ✓], [No week gaps or duplicates? ✓]. If span verification fails, STOP validation and return to drafting to complete missing weeks. This prevents deliverables ending prematurely at week 33 while asserting 52-week plans are complete.
[sai-00137] helpful=0 harmful=0 :: For manufacturing catch-up Excel deliverables with rolling backlog calculations: Implement BACKLOG MECHANICS CLARITY and FORMULA CONSISTENCY during MAPPING phase. (1) Establish a consistent sign convention for backlog values UPFRONT and document it explicitly in the Excel workbook. Choose either: (A) POSITIVE = BACKLOG (past-due/unfilled demand), NEGATIVE = BUFFER (completed ahead of schedule), or (B) POSITIVE = BUFFER, NEGATIVE = BACKLOG. (2) Once chosen, apply consistently throughout: Define the rolling balance formula explicitly: End-of-Week Backlog = Start-of-Week Backlog + Scheduled Demand − Weekly Capacity. Example with sign convention (A): [Week 4: Start = -438.81 (backlog), Demand = 100, Capacity = 180, End = -438.81 + 100 − 180 = -518.81 (increasing backlog is worsening, which is correct sign direction)]. (3) Verify week-to-week carryover: Start-of-Week(N) = End-of-Week(N−1). (4) Create a SIGN CONVENTION LEGEND section in the Excel workbook stating: 'Sign Convention: [State chosen convention]. Negative values indicate [backlog/buffer]; positive values indicate [buffer/backlog]. This convention is applied consistently across all weeks 4-52.' (5) During VALIDATION, spot-check at least 3 week sequences to verify: (A) Sign direction is consistent (backlog accumulation makes values more negative, not less), (B) Carryover formula is correct (start of week N equals end of week N−1), (C) The sign convention legend matches actual cell values. This prevents the error of inconsistent or confused backlog calculations that make catch-up analysis unreliable.

[sai-00161] helpful=1 harmful=0 :: For deliverable tasks requiring visual diagrams, flowcharts, or process maps in file formats (PDF, Visio, PNG): (1) TASK CATEGORIZATION: This is a DELIVERABLE TASK (not SPECIFICATION), even if you cannot natively generate the file format. The task requires an actual visual artifact with diagrammatic elements (nodes, connectors, lane boundaries, shapes), not text description of what the diagram would contain. (2) FRONTLOAD TRANSPARENCY IMMEDIATELY, before ANY drafting: 'This task requires a PDF file containing a visual process diagram with [list key visual elements: node shapes, decision diamonds, lane boundaries, connectors]. I cannot natively generate or embed graphics/PDF files. I will provide [Option A: Detailed Assembly Guide with step-by-step tool specifications] or [Option B: Complete Technical Specifications with exact node list, connector sequence, and coordinate positioning].' Do NOT provide text documentation as the deliverable—this is categorically insufficient for visual artifact tasks. (3) CRITICAL DISTINCTION: Text description of process flow ('Inbound pieces arrive → scanned → routed to automation/manual lanes based on compatibility classification') is NOT equivalent to a visual process diagram specification. Include sample node positioning and connector routing logic. (5) MAP ALL 31 CRITERIA TO DIAGRAM ELEMENTS BEFORE DRAFTING: Create a VISUAL-REQUIREMENT-TO-DIAGRAM-ELEMENT MATRIX mapping each criterion to specific diagram components. (6) DO NOT DRAFT until mapping is complete. Use the VISUAL-REQUIREMENT matrix as validation template: every mapped element MUST appear explicitly in your assembly guide or technical specifications. If any visual requirement cannot be mapped to a specific diagram element, STOP and reassess whether the deliverable is feasible under chosen approach. (7) ASSEMBLY GUIDE OR TECHNICAL SPECIFICATIONS (whichever approach you choose) must include: Complete enumeration of every node with shape type, label text, and positioning; complete enumeration of every connector with start/end node references, label text, and style (solid/dashed, arrow type); complete enumeration of both swim lanes with positioning (coordinates or relative placement), boundary styling, and header labels; sample formula or logic for decision routing (e.g., 'Decision Node routes to Automation Lane if [automation compatibility criteria met], else routes to Manual Lane'); exact tool navigation (e.g., 'In Lucidchart: Insert → Flowchart → Flowchart Terminator [dimensions 1.5×0.75], double-click to add label text "START: Inbound Receipt", position at [coordinates]'); exact PDF export settings (e.g., 'File → Export → PDF, set page size to Letter 8.5×11in, margins 0.5in, ensure all elements fit on single page or specify multi-page layout'). [Continue for all 31 criteria].' Only after this validation confirms all 31 criteria are explicitly addressed in your assembly guide or technical specifications should you provide the deliverable. [sai-00167] helpful=0 harmful=0 :: For testamentary-trust documents and estate-planning deliverables with minor-beneficiary provisions: Implement a TRUST DURATION LANGUAGE VERIFICATION GATE during MAPPING and VALIDATION phases. [sai-00173] helpful=0 harmful=0 :: For testamentary documents containing in-terrorem (no-contest) clauses: Treat the no-contest clause as a mandatory EXPLICIT ENUMERATION requirement that must appear as a complete, labeled clause with standard legal language. During DRAFTING, embed as a distinct, labeled section with complete in-terrorem language and exceptions. During VALIDATION, search for 'in terrorem' or 'no-contest' or 'contests the will' language and verify the full clause is present. In-terrorem clauses are commonly omitted in hastily-drafted wills; verification checkpoint prevents this omission.

[sai-00184] helpful=0 harmful=0 :: TASK CATEGORIZATION AND DELIVERY APPROACH BINDING GATE: When a task combines (A) imperative language ('provide CAD models', 'deliver 2D drawings', 'output STEP files'), (B) artifact-format specifications ('STEP file format', 'PDF', '.step or .stp extension'), and (C) rubric criteria tied to artifact delivery, this is DEFINITIVELY a DELIVERABLE task. Execute mandatory decision sequence: (1) Identify all three signals to confirm DELIVERABLE task type, (2) Document categorization explicitly with required outputs and verification gates, (3) Create BINDING DELIVERY APPROACH DECISION before content work begins, choosing between native generation, detailed assembly specifications, complete technical specifications, or alternative methods, (4) Treat this decision as binding through completion—any constraint discovered during execution requires conscious reassessment with explicit documentation, not silent pivoting. CRITICAL DISTINCTION: Recognize DELIVERABLE VERIFICATION GATES (imperative language: 'IS a Microsoft Word file', 'file contains', 'document is generated', 'provides PDF', 'ZIP archive contains') which verify ARTIFACT PRESENCE and FILE-FORMAT COMPLIANCE—these CANNOT be satisfied by descriptions, specifications, or alternative formats and require either the actual artifact in specified format OR detailed assembly specifications enabling reliable recreation. These are categorically distinct from CONTENT VERIFICATION GATES ('includes', 'addresses', 'discusses', 'explains', 'specifies') which verify CONTENT PRESENCE and can be satisfied by detailed content or technical specifications. When rubrics contain BOTH types (30+ gates using format-mandatory criteria like 'STEP file is provided', 'PDF drawing is generated', 'file is saved in [format]', 'artifact [type] is delivered', 'STEP format', 'PDF landscape', 'ANSI B landscape', 'title block format', 'BOM included', 'exploded view shown', 'balloons present', 'tolerances specified'), mark as [VERIFICATION GATE: ARTIFACT DELIVERY REQUIRED] and map format gates explicitly during MAPPING PHASE with ZERO TOLERANCE for approximation. Format gates are non-negotiable, binary (satisfied/unsatisfied), and must be addressed in BINDING DELIVERY APPROACH DECISION BEFORE DRAFTING BEGINS. Text descriptions fail all format-verification gates; only acceptable alternatives are: (A) Decline upfront if unable to generate required artifacts natively, or (B) Provide detailed specifications (assembly guide or technical specs) enabling user or CAD service to create actual artifacts. Never issue transparency statement about format limitations without immediately following with explicit BINDING DELIVERY APPROACH DECISION specifying which method addresses which constraints.
[sai-00192] helpful=0 harmful=0 :: For hard artifact tasks combining specific file format requirements and real-world sourced data (flight prices, hotel rates, availability), treat FORMAT DELIVERY and DATA SOURCING as orthogonal problems requiring separate decisions. Format constraints affect DELIVERY METHOD (formatted text + assembly specs, detailed specifications, or user-provided data population); data constraints affect DATA SOURCING METHOD (presupposition-based retrieval, public websites like Google Flights/booking sites, manual research). Critically: data-sourcing constraints do NOT eliminate artifact delivery—they require alternative sourcing strategies. Before concluding impossibility, systematically execute [sai-00094] alternative data sourcing checklist for EVERY required data element, attempting multiple methods (presupposition-based retrieval, public data websites, manual research) before declaring data inaccessible. Document attempts: 'Attempt 1: [method + result]. Attempt 2: [method + result]. Conclusion: [X]% of data available from alternative sources; creating best-effort deliverable with populated available data + NA markers for truly unavailable elements.' Data-sourcing constraints never justify zero output—they require alternative sourcing strategies and best-effort delivery with transparency about data currency and unavailable elements.

This prevents confusion in clinical documentation and ensures consistent terminology across providers.

[sai-00220] helpful=0 harmful=0 :: For journalistic and editorial article deliverables with NEUTRAL TONE requirements: Implement a NEUTRALITY VERIFICATION CHECKPOINT during VALIDATION phase, distinct from content completeness and enumeration verification. Neutral-tone requirements ('remains neutral, without expressing opinions about candidates and their election platforms', 'informative for voters regardless of political affiliation') are THEMATIC COVERAGE requirements that must be verified through language analysis, not just presence of certain content. During DRAFTING, apply neutrality guardrails: (A) Avoid modifiers suggesting preference or judgment ('exciting', 'historic', 'bold', 'reckless', 'promising', 'concerning')—use objective descriptors ('upcoming', 'scheduled', 'available'), (B) Avoid context-dependent framing that implies bias ('voters finally have a chance to', 'unfortunately', 'thankfully')—use neutral framing ('voters will have an opportunity to'), (C) Avoid candidate-comparative language ('the stronger candidate', 'the more experienced opponent', 'leading contender')—report only verified facts about election structure and dates, (D) Ensure any reference to candidates uses names or titles only, with no adjectives or characterizations. During VALIDATION NEUTRALITY CHECKPOINT: (1) Search article for adjectives describing candidates, parties, or positions—should find ZERO, (2) Search for modal verbs suggesting preference ('should', 'ought', 'must')—in neutral reporting, limit to procedural language only ('voters must register by [date]'), (3) Read article aloud and ask: 'Would a voter supporting any candidate or party feel this article favors a different position?'—if YES, revise to neutral language, (4) Search for absolute statements about election impact or significance ('will determine the state's future')—replace with factual statements about election scope only ('will elect the next governor'). If neutrality checkpoint detects bias or opinion language, STOP validation and revise article to remove editorializing before proceeding to format guidance. Neutral tone is a verification gate for journalistic articles, not optional enhancement.

[sai-00226] helpful=0 harmful=0 :: For literature-review deliverables with 10-15 page length requirements and enumerated sections (especially Results with 4+ subsections): Implement PROPORTIONAL SECTION-ALLOCATION AND DEPTH-MANAGEMENT strategy during MAPPING phase. If any enumeration item fails artifact verification, STOP and complete the artifact before proceeding. Never substitute specification text, template structure, or description for the actual working artifact.
